Stochastic transport equation with Lévy noise
Zdzisław Brzeźniak, Enrico Priola, Jianliang Zhai, Jiahui Zhu a . a. Department of Mathematics, University of York, York, UK b . b. Dipartimento di Matematica, Università di Pavia, via Ferrata 5, Pavia, Italy c . c. School of Mathematical Sciences, University of Science and Technology of China, Hefei, China d . d. School of Mathematical Sciences, Zhejiang University of Technology, Hangzhou, China

Abstract
We study the stochastic transport equation with globally β \beta -Hölder continuous and bounded vector field driven by a non-degenerate pure-jump Lévy noise of α \alpha -stable type. Whereas the deterministic transport equation may lack uniqueness, we prove the existence and pathwise uniqueness of a weak solution in the presence of a multiplicative pure jump noise, assuming α 2 + β > 1 \frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta>1 . Notably, our results cover the entire range α ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \alpha\in(0,2) , including the supercritical regime α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) where the driving noise exhibits notoriously weak regularization. A key step of our strategy is the development of a sharp C 1 + δ C^{1+\delta} -diffeomorphism and new regularity results for the Jacobian determinant of the stochastic flow associated to its stochastic characteristic equation. These novel probabilistic results are of independent interest and constitute a substantial component of our work. Our results are the first full generalization of the celebrated paper by Flandoli, Gubinelli, and Priola [Invent. Math. 2010] from the Brownian motion to the pure jump Lévy noise. To the best of our knowledge, this appears to be the first example of a partial differential equation of fluid dynamics where well-posedness is restored by the influence of a non-degenerate pure-jump noise. Keywords: Stochastic singular transport equation, Multiplicative pure jump noise, Regularization by noise, Pathwise uniqueness, Stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms. Mathematics Subject Classification(2020) : 60H17, 60H50, 60G51, 35R25, 35R05



1
Introduction

In this article, we study the following two main questions. Firstly, we study
the well-posedness of the following singular linear transport equation with multiplicative gradient type noises of jump type

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{u(t,x)}}{\partial t}+b(x)\cdot Du(t,x)\,+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}u(t-,x)\diamond\frac{dL_{t}^{i}}{dt}=0,~t\in[0,T],\;\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(1.1)

\displaystyle u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(1.2)

Here we assume that

D_{i}
is the weak partial derivative in the
i
-th direction,

b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a vector field which is bounded and Hölder continuous with exponent
\beta\in(0,1)
, i.e.,

b\in C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d});

(1.3)

and

L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a non-degenerate
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued
pure-jump Lévy process defined on a probability space
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})

of
\alpha
-stable type with
\alpha\in(0,2)
, see also
the monograph

[
82
]
. Equation (
1.1
) is written in the Markus form; for an equivalent formulation, see equation (
5.23
) in Remark
5.7
. In this work, we primarily deal with the so called weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solutions, see Definition
5.8
. Moreover, in Appendix
C
we also consider the case of classical
C^{1}
-solutions.

Secondly, we are interested in the
regularity properties of the solution to the following stochastic ordinary differential equation (SDE),
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
s\in[0,\infty)
,
driven by the process
L
:

\displaystyle dX_{t}

\displaystyle=b(X_{t})\,dt+dL_{t},\;t>s;\;\;\;

(1.4)

\displaystyle X_{s}

\displaystyle=x,

which is the stochastic characteristics equation for equation (
1.1
).

Note that when
L=0
, the corresponding transport equation may have infinitely many solutions, see the discussion before equation (
1.5
). In contrast, we prove in Theorems
6.16
and
6.18
that equation (
1.1
) becomes well-posed provided the Lévy noise
L
is
(in suitable sense) non-degenerate.
For clarity and ease of presentation, we present below Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
which are simplified versions of the well posedness Theorems
6.16
and
6.18
to equation (
1.1
).

One of the reason we consider the transport equation (
1.1
) in the Markus canonical form is that it has a solution represented in terms of the stochastic flow associated with equation (
1.4
)

u(t,x,\omega):=u_{0}\big(\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(\omega)(x)\big),\quad t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
is the inverse flow of equation (
1.4
) with
s=0
.
Consequently, a thorough understanding of the
fine properties
of this flow is essential.
Let us point out that to the best of our knowledge, there is no work that has explored
the
sharp

C^{1+\delta}
-diffeomorphism property of the flows, leaving a significant gap between the existing results and the more refined outcomes we target. This motivates our second main objective and constitutes another central contribution of the present paper, which is also of independent interest.

Our results concerning the first question demonstrate the so-called
regularization by noise
phenomenon in the context of Lévy processes. This represents, to the best of our knowledge, the first example of a PDE of fluid dynamics that becomes well-posed under the influence of a pure-jump Lévy noise.

The analysis of the pure-jump case also leads us to develop new analytical and probabilistic techniques, which we believe will be useful beyond the present framework.

Motivation for the study of stochastic transport equations.

Let us spend some time discussing the motivation for this problem.

A transport equation is a basic partial differential equation (PDE) in fluid dynamics.
A vast literature has been devoted to the well-posedness of the deterministic transport equations
with singular time-dependent vector fields
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
A remarkable example is a result obtained by Di Perna and Lions in
[
35
]
, who proved that, if the time dependent vector field
b\in L^{1}(0,T;W_{loc}^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfies the linear growth condition with respect to the spatial variable and
{\mathrm{div}\,}b\in L^{1}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, then for every given
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists a unique
L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

weak-
\ast
solution that is continuous in time. Moreover, a generalized notion of a flow was introduced and its existence and uniqueness were proved.
For more information, see a partial review
[
2
]
, the recent paper

[
32
]
and the references therein.

On the other hand, it is well-known that when
L=0
, i.e., without noise, there exist counterexamples to the uniqueness of weak-
\ast
solutions even in dimension
d=1
. For instance, see Section 6.1 in
[
39
]
, for an arbitrary
\gamma\in\left(0,1\right)
and
R>0
, one can consider the following vector field
b
on
\mathbb{R}
:

b(x)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\,\mathrm{sign}\,(x)\left(|x|\wedge R\right)^{\gamma},\;\;x\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.5)

These singular coefficients can lead to non-uniqueness of solutions to the corresponding deterministic transport equations.

In contrast, by introducing suitable stochastic noise, the problem becomes well-posed, i.e., both the existence and the uniqueness of solutions hold, a phenomenon known as
regularization by noise
.
This effect has attracted considerable interest from both physicists and experts in stochastic analysis.

For the specific transport equation considered in this work, the foundational study can be traced to the pioneering work
[
39
]
, by Flandoli, Gubinelli and the second named author, which established the well-posedness results in the case of Brownian Motion. Subsequent research has significantly expanded this framework, investigating the Brownian noise scenario under various assumptions on the singularity of the drift coefficient
b
and the initial data
u_{0}
; see, for example,
[
10
,
38
,
44
,
53
]
and
[
92
]
. Parallel developments have also been made for equations driven by fractional Brownian Motion, as documented in
[
3
,
25
]
, and the references therein. It is important to note that all the aforementioned works are confined to the continuous noise case. In Remark
1.7
, we will discuss a random transport equation involving Lévy noise
considered in a recent paper
[
46
]
. It turns out that the equation studied in that paper is different from our equation
(
1.1
).

Regarding the general phenomenon of regularization by noise, we refer the reader to the following literature: for results on Brownian motion-driven ordinary differential equations (ODEs), see
[
57
,
93
,
94
]
; for the stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) driven by Brownian motion, see
[
5
,
7
,
42
,
48
]
; and for ODEs driven by jump noise, see
[
6
,
31
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
; and
[
26
]
for other driving noises.

The present paper presents the first study for SPDEs driven by jump noise in this direction. Compared with the existing literature, our analysis of the pure-jump case necessitates the development of new analytical techniques, which will be introduced and discussed in detail later.

We next discuss the motivation for the use of a jump noise. Although continuous type
noises have been regarded as a natural choice for modeling
random noise in continuous-time systems, numerous examples and evidence of jump type
noises have been discovered and documented in many real world complex systems. The monograph

[
13
]
presents a phenomenological study of fully developed turbulence and intermittency;
it proposes that experimental observations of these physical
characteristics of fluid dynamics can be modeled by stochastic PDEs with Lévy noise.
We should mention here two recent papers about the fluid dynamics driven by Lévy processes, i.e.,
[
21
]
and
[
22
]
, where a fair attempt has been made to describe earlier contributions in that field.
The theory and several applications of
SPDEs with Lévy noise have been considered in the monograph
[
75
]
.

These empirical and theoretical advances motivate the incorporation of pure jump
Lévy noise into the transport problem. Such a formulation allows us to capture
anomalous transport and fluctuation effects that are beyond the scope of classical
diffusion models.

We also point out that the statistics of the Lévy type noise turned out to be ubiquitous phenomena empirically observed in various areas including: physics (anomalous
diffusion, turbulent flows, nonlinear Hamiltonian dynamics
[
52
,
85
]
), biology (e.g., heartbeats
[
74
]
and firing of neural networks
[
84
]
), seismology (e.g., recordings of seismic activity
[
87
]
), electrical engineering (e.g., signal
processing
[
51
,
66
,
91
]
), mathematical finance (
[
33
]
and
[
9
]
), and economics (e.g., financial time series
[
16
,
67
,
68
]
).
Accordingly, an important problem is to understand the dynamics of
stochastic systems when the driving noises are Lévy type. The present work represents a step toward this goal.

Let us now concentrate on the motivation for working with equations in the Marcus canonical form. It is well-known that the Itô integral, despite its many remarkable features, fails to satisfy the classical chain rule and therefore Itô SDEs are not invariant under changes of coordinates. As a consequence, from a physical point of view, it does not provide a natural geometric framework. In the Brownian motion driven systems, these drawbacks can often be overcome by using the Stratonovich integral instead of the Itô one. Marcus canonical form is specifically designed as a natural extension of the Stratonovich calculus to the jump processes. Moreover, stochastic differential equations written in the Marcus canonical form possess the geometric invariance under the change of variables, which yields a structural property that is crucial for our purposes. In particular, we prove that under some regularity assumptions, the unique weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution to (
1.1
) is given by a formula (
1.9
), which involves the stochastic flow generated by the equation (
1.4
) (and associated with (
1.1
)), see also the discussion surrounding that formula. This flow representation plays a central role in our uniqueness argument for the stochastic transport equation. On the other hand, the Marcus canonical integral can be interpreted as the limit of Wong-Zakai type approximations, where the jump noise is replaced by a sequence of smoother (piecewise smooth) processes. From physical and modeling points of view, this perspective is also useful for numerical method approximating solutions of SDEs, see for instance
[
63
]
and
[
73
]
, where SDEs with jumps with regular coefficients are discussed. For studies on stochastic (partial) differential equations involving the Marcus integral and their background, we also direct readers to the seminal work
[
69
]
and to the recent developments found in
[
18
,
29
,
43
,
47
,
65
]
.

Our first main results: The well-posedness results for (
1.1
).

In order to simplify the presentation of this Introduction, here we only state our results in the case where the Lévy process
L
is a rotationally invariant
\alpha
-stable process whose generator is the fractional Laplacian
(-\triangle)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, i.e., when
L
satisfies our Hypothesis
3
. This is a special but yet interesting case.

More general Lévy processes are considered in Section
2.3
, where we introduce three different assumptions on
L
, see Hypotheses
1
,
2
and

3
. These hypotheses are crucial for our main results. Roughly speaking, in the supercritical case
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have to assume that
L
is rotationally invariant but
when
\alpha\in[1,2)
we can consider more general non-degenerate
\alpha
-stable-type processes
L
.

Let us first discuss the existence of solutions to (
1.1
), see Theorem
5.10
for more details, which holds under more general assumptions on
L
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
\alpha\in(0,2)
and

\frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta>1.

(1.6)

Assume that
L
is a rotationally invariant
\alpha
-stable Lévy process whose
symbol
\psi
is of the following form,

\displaystyle\psi(u)=C_{\alpha}|u|^{\alpha},\;\;u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(1.7)

for some constant
C_{\alpha}>0
, and a vector field
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies conditions (
1.3
) and

\operatorname{div}b\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(1.8)

Then for every Borel measurable and essentially bounded function
u_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, the stochastic process
u
defined by the following formula

u(t,x,\omega):=u_{0}\left(\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(\omega)(x)\right),\;\;t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\omega\in\Omega,

(1.9)

where
(\phi_{0,t})_{t\geq 0}
is the stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms associated to the SDE (
1.4
) with
s=0
and
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
is its inverse flow,
is a weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution to the problem (
1.1
) in the sense of Definition
5.8
.

As we clearly see, the above result is not self-contained as the stochastic flow
(\phi_{0,t})_{t\geq 0}
appears in formula (
1.9
). As stated before, it is related to the second main question of the paper, and it will be addressed in Theorem
1.6
, which presents a refined version of the result.

Now we state two main uniqueness results. The first uniqueness result holds under the condition (
1.6
) but
it assumes that
\alpha\in[1,2)
. It can be proved for more general Lévy processes, and the complete statement can be found in Theorem
6.16
.
The critical exponent
p\in(\alpha,2]
is also included in this theorem. In contrast to the uniqueness result for Brownian motion, in
[
41
, Theorem 6]
, our critical range is independent of the spatial dimension
d
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let us assume that
\alpha\in[1,2)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
are such that (
1.6
) holds.

Assume also that one of the following conditions holds.

If
d=1
, then
Db\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
; if
d\geq 2
, then
\operatorname{div}b\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
p\geq 2
.

If
d=1
, then
Db\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
; if
d\geq 2
, then
\beta>1-\alpha+\frac{\alpha}{p}
and
\operatorname{div}b\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
p\in(\alpha,2]
.
Then, for every
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists a unique weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution to the
transport equation. It is
of the form (
1.9
).

The next uniqueness result is quite unexpected because it also holds in the
supercritical case

\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Recall that in this case the generator of the Lévy process
L
is the fractional Laplacian of order
\frac{\alpha}{2}<\frac{1}{2}
. This result shows regularization by noise even in the case of
\alpha\in(0,1)
. The complete statement of this result can be found in Theorem
6.18
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
are such that

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{4}+\beta>1.

(1.10)

Assume also that condition (
1.8
) holds. Then, for every
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists a unique weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution to the transport equation.
It is
of the form (
1.9
).

Remark 1.4
.

In a similar way, we can treat the more general case of the time-dependent drift vector field
b(t,x)
. But this would substantially enlarge the paper and thus we have decided
to postpone the treatment of time-dependent drift to a future publication.

Remark 1.5
.

Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
differ primarily in two aspects. First, the function space for
\operatorname{div}b
differs: one requires

L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
p>1
, while the other assumes
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Second, the relationship between the parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
is given by equations (
1.6
) and (
1.10
), respectively. This distinction is natural: since Theorem
1.3
imposes a weaker condition on
\operatorname{div}b
, it correspondingly requires stronger assumptions elsewhere.

In order to prove the above theorems, we need a couple of new results of the flow associated with (
1.4
).

Our second main result: Existence and stability of stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms.

We obtain the following
fine properties
of the flow associated with SDE (
1.4
), see Theorems
3.23
and
4.1
for more details, which is not only essential for formulating the first problem but also of great importance in itself.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let us assume that
T>0
,
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
satisfy condition (
1.6
).
Then there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subset\Omega
and a map

\phi:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\;

here
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
is independent of
(s,t,x)\in[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that, for every
(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the process

[s,T]\times\Omega\ni(t,\omega)\mapsto\phi_{s,t}(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

is the unique solution to SDE
(
1.4
) and for all
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
,
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
,
the mapping

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\phi_{s,t}(x)=\phi_{s,t}(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1.11)

is a surjective diffeomorphism of class
C^{1+\delta}
for any
\delta\in[0,\frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta-1)
. More precisely, both
\phi_{s,t}(x)
and its inverse
\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(x)
satisfy the sharp càdlàg property in
(s,t,x)
separately:

for every
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
,
the maps

(s,t,x)\mapsto\phi_{s,t}(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\quad(s,t,x)\mapsto\phi^{-1}_{s,t}(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

are càdlàg w.r.t.
s
, for all
(t,x)
fixed, càdlàg w.r.t.
t
, for all
(s,x)
fixed, and continuous w.r.t.
x
, for all
(s,t)
fixed;
moreover, for every
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta-1)
,
the derivative functions

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto D\phi_{s,t}(x)\in\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto D(\phi_{s,t}^{-1})(x)\in\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})

are locally
\delta
-Hölder continuous uniformly in
(s,t)\in[0,T]\times[0,T]
;
and

D\phi_{s,t}(x),D(\phi_{s,t}^{-1})(x)

exhibit the sharp càdlàg property in
(s,t,x)
separately.

Let
(b^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})

be a sequence of vector fields and
\phi^{n}
the corresponding
stochastic flows.
Let
b^{n}\to b
in
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Then, for every
p\geq 1
and any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[s,T]}|\phi_{s,t}^{n}(x)-\phi_{s,t}(x)|^{p}]=0;

(1.12)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[s,T]}\|D\phi_{s,t}^{n}(x)\|^{p}]+\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[s,T]}\|D\phi_{s,t}(x)\|^{p}]<\infty;

(1.13)

\displaystyle\,\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in K}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[s,T]}\|D\phi_{s,t}^{n}(x)-D\phi_{s,t}(x)\|^{p}]=0.

(1.14)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|(\phi_{s,t}^{n})^{-1}(x)-\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(x)|^{p}]=0,

(1.15)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|D(\phi_{s,t}^{n})^{-1}(x)\|^{p}]+\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|D\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(x)\|^{p}]<\infty,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\,\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in K}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|D(\phi_{s,t}^{n})^{-1}(x)-D\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(x)\|^{p}]=0.

(1.17)

New contributions of the paper.

In what follows, we highlight
our main new contributions
of the current paper. Items (i) and (ii) are related to the second main result of the paper.

(i)

The proof of regularization property of the stochastic flow. We show that the flow associate to the characteristic equation (
1.4
) is a
C^{1+\delta}
-diffeomorphism for Hölder continuous coefficients, see Theorem
1.6
. The requirement that the
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
is independent of
(s,t,x)\in[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is critical, introducing inherent technical challenges.

(ii)

New regularity of the Jacobian determinant of the solution to (
1.4
) with respect to
x
. This result requires delicate results on the Kolmogorov equations, the use of stochastic integrals in Banach spaces of martingale type
2
, and sophisticated estimates in fractional Sobolev spaces (cf., Theorems
6.13
and
6.14
).

(iii)

The linking between (i), (ii) and the above uniqueness theorems on the transport equation is achieved via
the crucial Itô-Wentzell formula in the jump case. To the best of our knowledge, there is only one paper that rigorously applies the Itô-Wentzell formula in the jump case, see
[
64
]
, which is based on the seminal paper
[
71
]
. In our setting with singular coefficient
b
, a rigorous application and verification of the Itô-Wentzell formula require a meticulous and careful calculation.

(iv)

The pathwise uniqueness of weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solutions to the linear transport equation (
1.1
),
with the initial data
u_{0}
being an
essentially bounded
Borel measurable function, are established for
\beta
-Hölder continuous drifts,
\beta\in(0,1)
, and
\alpha
-stable type processes,
\alpha\in(0,2)
, including the supercritical case, i.e.,
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

We now describe these main contributions in more detail.

Firstly, the method of constructing solutions analogous to the transport equation (
1.1
) is referred to as the method of characteristics. This requires starting from the stochastic characteristics equation (
1.4
) for the transport equation (
1.1
). In the stochastic case, it is essential to study the fine properties of the stochastic characteristic equation, i.e., the flow of equation (
1.4
), as detailed in Theorem
1.6
, which is foundational for our analysis. The established theory of stochastic flows for SDEs driven by jump processes, even for regular Lipschitz coefficients, is insufficient for our purposes. The latest existing results on this topic are summarized/discussed below:

•

The
sharp càdlàg property
(scp) for the flow and its inverse, which is crucial for defining our solutions via the method of characteristics, remains unaddressed in all prior works. For instance, Kunita in his recent monograph on jump diffusions
[
62
]
considers possibly degenerate SDEs with coefficients
satisfying the Lipschitz conditions but does not prove, or even mention the scp for the flow and its inverse.
Meanwhile,
[
79
]
(corresponding to Theorem
3.5
in this paper) examines the case of singular drift
b
with non-degenerate stable noise but only addresses the scp of the flow itself, neglecting the scp for the inverse flow.
This scp is discussed in more detail in a recent paper
[
15
]
by Bondi and the second named author, and they proved the scp for the flow by independent methods for possibly degenerate SDEs with Lipschitz coefficients, but they also do not study the scp for the inverse flow, see Remark
1.8
and the comment at the end of page 7 in
[
15
]
for more details.

•

When the vector
b
is singular, the authors of
[
30
]
,
[
76
]
and
[
77
]
investigate the homeomorphism property and differentiability with respect to the spatial variable for solutions of equation (
1.4
) for
\alpha\in[1,2)
and
\alpha\in(0,2)
, respectively, see Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
in this paper. However, the existence of a
\mathbb{P}
-full set independent of
s,t
and the
C^{1}
and
C^{1+\delta}
-diffeomorphism property of the flow (
1.11
) have not been studied. The requirement of a
s,t
-independent
\mathbb{P}
-full set is essential for resolving our problems.

In contrast to these existing results, our result described in Theorem
1.6
, not only establishes the scp for the flow and its inverse but also demonstrates that their spatial derivatives exhibit the scp. A key contribution is that we prove the existence of a full-
\mathbb{P}
event, independent of
s,t,x
, on which the stochastic flow
\phi_{s,t}(x)
associated with our SDE (
1.4
), is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism. Furthermore, we establish that its derivatives
D\phi_{s,t}(x)
and
D\phi^{-1}_{s,t}(x)
are locally
\delta
-Hölder continuous in
x
and exhibit sharp càdlàg properties in the time parameters separately.
As highlighted earlier, there is a significant gap between the existing results and what is required for our purposes. Obtaining these precise and reliable results poses considerable challenges in our specific setting. The principal obstacles/challenges are summarized as follows:

•

The
\rm It\hat{o}
-Tanaka approach, which is similar
to the Zvonkin transformation, plays a central role in the study of the
fine properties
of the flow associated with (
1.4
). Heuristically, this approach transfers the regularization of the law of the diffusion associated with a corresponding parabolic/Kolmogorov equation to its sample paths.
In our setting, the associated parabolic (Kolmogorov) equation takes the form

\displaystyle\lambda v-{\mathscr{A}}v-b\cdot Dv=f,\;\;\lambda>0,

(1.18)

where
{\mathscr{A}}
is the generator of the pure jump Lévy process
L
. In this case
{\mathscr{A}}
is a nonlocal operator. For the simplicity of presentation, we assume as before that
L
is a rotationally invariant
\alpha
-stable process whose generator is the fractional Laplacian
(-\triangle)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
. When
\alpha\in(1,2)
, the leading term in (
1.18
) is
{\mathscr{A}}v
. Here, by leading term we mean the term that dominates the regularization effect in (
1.18
). When
\alpha=1
,
{\mathscr{A}}v
and
Dv
have the same order and both serve as leading terms, and it is necessary to take into account the interaction between these two terms. In contrast, when
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the leading term comes from
Dv
, while
{\mathscr{A}}v
, capturing the driving noise’s regularization effect, plays a subordinate role. In this supercritical case, observing the regularization by noise phenomenon becomes extremely difficult.

For comparison, when the noise is Brownian motion,
{\mathscr{A}}
is the Laplacian (corresponding to
\alpha=2
). Clearly, the jump noise in our setting produces weaker regularization than the Brownian motion case. Additionally, the Brownian motion case involves a local operator, whereas our framework deals with a nonlocal operator. As a result, the analysis of all of the main results in our setting becomes considerably more challenging, especially for
\alpha\in(0,1)
. This explains why obtaining the uniqueness result in the supercritical case is somewhat unexpected. Indeed, the regularization by noise phenomenon in supercritical case still contains several open problems, see Remark
3.3
for related discussions. Finally, we note that, due to these analytical difficulties, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
our results yield only the local stability of the flow, see (
1.14
), (
1.17
), (
3.50
), Remarks
3.18
and
4.5
. Nevertheless, this is still sufficient to establish the uniqueness of the solutions to the transport equation (
1.1
).

•

The proof of this diffeomorphism property is substantially more involved than its Brownian motion counterpart and requires new analytical tools. In order to establish the
sharp

C^{1+\delta}
-diffeomorphism property of the stochastic flow
\phi_{s,t}
, the core of our analysis lies in a careful investigation of the properties of
\phi_{0,t}(x)
, its inverse mapping
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(x)
and their spatial derivatives. The flow and its inverse satisfy, respectively, the following stochastic differential equations:

\displaystyle\phi_{0,t}(x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(\phi_{0,r}(x))dr+L_{t},\quad t\geq 0;

(1.19)

\displaystyle\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(x)=x-\int_{0}^{t}b(\phi_{r,t}^{-1}(x))dr-L_{t},\;\;t\geq 0.

(1.20)

Even though equations (
1.19
) and (
1.20
) appear structurally similar, a closer examination reveals that they rely on fundamentally different analytical structures. In equation (
1.19
), the drift term
b(\phi_{0,r}(x))
depends on the forward flow
\phi_{0,r}(x)
, which is the central subject of our study. This structure makes it possible to adapt classical arguments; however, their application is nontrivial due to the singularity of

b
and the limited regularization effect of the driving noise
L
. In contrast, equation (
1.20
) is an SDE in the backward time direction, where
b(\phi_{r,t}^{-1}(x))
involves the inverse flow
\phi_{r,t}^{-1}
,
r\in[0,t]
, not
\phi_{0,r}^{-1}
. Such a structural difference makes the analysis of
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
and, in particular, its derivative substantially more delicate. While one may formally write
b(\phi_{r,t}^{-1}(x))
as
b(\phi_{0,r}\circ\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(x))
, such a representation does not simplify the analysis, as
\phi_{0,r}(y),y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a family of stochastic processes. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce a new auxiliary stochastic process
K
that characterizes the derivative of the inverse flow through a representation identity, see (
3.107
) and (
3.108
). Importantly, the process
K
possesses better regularity properties, making the analysis of
D\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
considerably more tractable. This construction is highly non-trivial, as it relies on a combination of the Itô-Tanaka trick, the inverse mapping theorem and a strengthened version of the Kolmogorov test. By means of these analytical ingredients, we derive a path-wise formulation for the derivative of the inverse flow
D\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
, see (
3.157
). This approach not only yields a rigorous representation of
D\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
but also provides the key tool for proving the local Hölder continuity and sharp càdlàg property of the derivative of the inverse stochastic flow i.e., the
sharp càdlàg property
of
D\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(x)
in
(s,t,x)
separately.

•

Let us emphasize again that the above properties hold on a full-
\mathbb{P}
event
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
which is independent of
s,t
and
x
. This aspect gives rise to the third major challenge and highlights another key distinction between our result and the existing results. The independence of
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
from
s,t,x
is crucial for our analysis and is repeatedly used throughout the entire proof. Establishing such
\mathbb{P}
-full set independent of
s,t
is considerably more involved than dealing with fixed values of
s
and
t
. It requires delicate analytical arguments and the development of new technical ingredients, see for instance, the proofs of Lemma
3.6
, Theorem
3.19
and Theorem
3.23
.

Secondly, with the non-regular vector fields
b
, our uniqueness result for equation (
1.1
) is proved by means of the concept of renormalized solutions and commutator lemmas. This approach requires a detailed analysis of the Jacobian determinant of the stochastic flow, denoted by
J\phi_{0,t}
, which can be written as

\log J\phi_{0,t}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{div}b\left(\phi_{0,s}(x)\right)ds.

(1.21)

Since the drift
b
is only
\beta
-Hölder continuous with some
\beta\in(0,1)
, we must explore the improved regularity of the integral term of the right-hand side in order to establish the desired properties of the Jacobian. We again employ the Itô-Tanaka trick and the regularizing effect of the associated nonlocal parabolic equation (
1.18
), see in particular Theorem
6.10
, which is based on results by
[
95
]
. In comparison with the Brownian motion case, see
[
39
, Theorem 10]
, where the
L^{p}
-parabolic estimates
\|v\|_{W^{2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is used, our nonlocal parabolic equation (
1.18
) has poor regularity:
\|v\|_{H^{\alpha,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, with only
\alpha\in[1,2)
. Here
v
is the unique solution of equation (
1.18
) with RHS
f
. Specifically, when
\alpha\in(1,2)
, our analysis is confined to fractional Sobolev spaces
H^{\alpha,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since
\alpha
is not an integer, several classical embeddings fail. For instance, even for a bounded domain
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the embedding
W^{\delta,p}(\mathcal{O})\subset W^{\delta,1}(\mathcal{O})
,
p>1
no longer holds when
\delta
is not an integer, see
[
72
]
. Because of weaker regularizing effect of our nonlocal parabolic equation and the failure of certain Sobolev embeddings, we can only prove that
J\phi_{0,t}
belongs to a local fractional-order Sobolev space
W_{r}^{\delta,p}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
and
p>1
. See Theorems
6.13
and
6.14
for a complete description. The analysis within our framework poses some substantial technical challenges. Indeed, we develop new regularity results, i.e., Theorems
6.13
and
6.14
, concerning the fractional Sobolev‑type regularity of the Jacobian determinant. The proofs of these theorems rely on delicate analyses of Kolmogorov equations. Moreover, we employ a tool different from
[
39
]
, namely estimates for stochastic integrals with respect to compensated Poisson random measures in Banach spaces of martingale type 2; see
[
97
]
and the references therein. Our argument also relies on sophisticated estimates in fractional Sobolev spaces, see e.g., Lemma
6.12
, (
6.55
), (
6.56
), (
6.59
) and (
6.71
). These techniques do not arise in the Brownian motion case, as the Jacobian’s behavior in jump settings introduces additional complexities that are absent in the Brownian Motion case.

Thirdly, we bridge the existence of the regular flow with the well-posedness of the transport equation via the Itô-Wentzell formula in the jump case. While prior work
[
64
]
provides a rigorous foundation, its application to our SPDEs context is non-trivial. Verifying its assumptions and reformulating it into a form suitable for our specific needs, as detailed in the appendices, constitutes a further contribution. To be more specific, we briefly recall the underlying problem and illustrate the formula in our framework. Let

F(t,y):=u^{\varepsilon}(t,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}(y-x)u(t,x)\,dx,\quad\quad\xi_{t}=\phi_{0,t}(x),

where
\varepsilon>0
and
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
is a standard mollifier,
u
is a weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution to the transport equation and
\phi_{0,t}
is the associated stochastic flow. For each fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the process
F(t,y)
satisfies SDE (
6.90
), and
\xi_{t}
satisfies (
1.4
) with
s=0
. The Itô-Wentzell formula investigates the conditions under which the composition process
F(t,\xi_{t})
is well defined and yields the explicit stochastic dynamics governing its evolution. A major difficulty lies in the random process
F(t,y)
, which satisfies the jump-driven SDE (
6.90
) for each fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, only on
y
-dependent
\mathbb{P}
-full set. To rigorously define the composition process
F(t,\xi_{t})
, we must establish a universal
\mathbb{P}
-full set independent of
y
. This necessitates a stronger form of regularity of the random field
F(t,y)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Specifically, we must establish the existence of a
\mathbb{P}
-full event, on which the trajectory of
F(t,y)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
belongs to
D([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}))
. In other words, on this
\mathbb{P}
-full event, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
F(t,y)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is twice continuously differentiable in space and is a
C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
-valued càdlàg function w.r.t.
t\in[0,T]
; see Lemma
B.1
for the proof. In addition, one must carefully check the relation between jumps of the processes
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto F(t,y)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and those for
\xi_{t}
such as establishing

\displaystyle\sum_{s\leq T}\;\sup_{y\in\mathbb{K}}|D_{y}F(s,y)-D_{y}F(s-,y)||\xi_{s}-\xi_{s-}|<\infty,\quad\mathbb{K}=[-K,K]^{d},

(1.22)

and analyzing sums of the form

\sum_{s\leq t}\Big(\Delta F(s,\xi_{s})-\Delta F(s,\xi_{s-})\Big),\quad\quad here\ \Delta F(s,y):=F(s,y)-F(s-,y).

We refer to Appendix
B
, in particular (
B.31
) and (
B.67
), for the details.
Handling these interactions requires a careful analysis of the jump structures of càdlàg paths.
Consequently, such a formula is a very delicate issue. To clarify this point, we first include Appendix
A
in which we provide a simplified reformulation of Proposition 4.1 from the very nice paper
[
64
]
by Leahy and Mikulevicius, as well as correct some small its inaccuracies. We would like to emphasize that even the simplified form of the Itô-Wentzell formula
given in Appendix
A

is quite difficult to apply in concrete
examples related to SPDEs. Indeed, as mentioned above, it is a challenging task to verify whether the several assumptions of the Itô-Wentzell formula in the jump case
hold and also to simplify such a formula in a convenient way. In a second step, in Appendix
B
, we verify that the assumptions of our result from Appendix
A
are indeed satisfied in the case of

u^{\varepsilon}(t,y),y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\phi_{0,t}(x)
. This procedure plays a crucial role in establishing the uniqueness of the stochastic transport equation, see the proofs of Theorems
6.16
and
6.18
. Overall, the situation appears to be very different with respect to the one in the well-known Itô-Wentzell formula in the continuous case, see e.g., the monograph
[
60
]
and the proof of Theorem 20 in
[
39
]
.

It is worth mentioning that even the definition of weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution requires special attention in the present jump case, see in particular Section
5.1
, Remark
5.4
and Lemma
5.3
. As mentioned earlier, we consider the so-called weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solutions of the linear transport type equation (
1.1
),
with the initial data
u_{0}
being an
essentially bounded
Borel measurable function and the stochastic integration is understood in the Marcus form, see e.g.,
[
4
]
and
[
80
, page 82]
.
The weak form of this equation is obtained by (formally) integrating against test functions
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
assuming some integrability conditions on
\operatorname{div}b
as in
[
39
]
; see Definition
5.6
in Section
5
. We stress that our definition of a weak solution already requires overcoming some technical difficulties, which are not present in the Brownian Motion case considered in
[
39
]
, see in particular the discussion at the beginning of Section
5
and Differences
5.1
. In particular, the Marcus form of equation (
1.1
) can be rewritten using the Itô stochastic integrals with respect to compensated Poisson random measures, see Definition
5.8
and compare this to the Stratonovich equation in the Brownian Motion case, see
[
19
]
.

Finally, after overcoming the above difficulties, the pathwise uniqueness of weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution is established under the condition
b\in C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\beta\in(0,1)
and
\operatorname{div}b\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
p=1
with
\alpha\in(0,2)
and some
p\in(\alpha,2)\cup[2,\infty)
with
\alpha\in[1,2)
, including the critical interval
p\in(\alpha,2]
.
Our pathwise uniqueness results reveal a regularization by noise phenomenon in the setting of Lévy-driven SPDEs, as without noise there are examples of non-uniqueness under the above weak conditions on
b
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first rigorous demonstration of such an effect within the Lévy-driven SPDEs framework.

Importantly, we also prove in Appendix
D
that the definition of a weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution can be formulated equivalently without using the Marcus canonical integral. The proof in our jump case is delicate. We believe that this result is of independent interest. It is in the spirit of rough path theory. For recent developments of rough path theory for SDEs driven by Lévy processes, see
[
55
]
and the references therein.
In addition, we establish a no blow-up result of
C^{1}
solutions in Appendix
C
. Its proof again requires the use of the Itô-Wentzell formula in the jump case.
This result is related to the one proved in
[
41
]
in the case of Brownian motion, see also
[
37
]
.

It is an open problem to extend the two Wong-Zakai approximation results
proved in Appendix C of
[
39
]
to our jump case.
We plan to address this problem in future work.

Plan of the paper and additional comments.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
is dedicated to preliminaries on Lévy processes, function spaces, and our main assumptions. Section
3
is devoted to the study of the stochastic flow, where the regularity results are established. Section
4
presents stability estimates essential for subsequent analysis, with particular emphasis on the case
0<\alpha<1
. Section
5
proves the main existence theorem for the transport equation, with a rough version of that result already formulated in Theorem
1.1
. Two main uniqueness results for the transport equation, see Theorems
6.16
and
6.18
, are proved in Section
6
. To establish these uniqueness results, we also derive two new fractional Sobolev type regularity results concerning the Jacobian determinant of the stochastic flow, namely Theorems
6.13
and
6.14
.

The Itô-Wentzell formula in the jump case is presented in Appendix
A
. In Appendix
B
, we verify several assumptions of the Itô-Wentzell formula for
u^{\varepsilon}
and
\phi_{0,t}
and apply it to our problem. We prove a result of no blow-up of
C^{1}
solutions in Appendix
C
. Appendix
D
shows that the definition of weak
∗
-
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-solution can be formulated equivalently without using the Marcus integration form. Finally, Section
E
contains the proof of some technical lemmata, and Section
F
reviews the Kolmogorov-Totoki-Cencov-type theorem
in a form we need in the paper.

Remark 1.7
.

We mention that
[
46
]
investigates the well-posedness of classical solutions to the following random transport equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{u(t,x,\omega)}}{\partial t}+(b(t,x)+L_{t}(\omega))\cdot Du(t,x,\omega)\,=0,~t>0;

(1.23)

\displaystyle u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(1.24)

The authors assume that
u_{0}\in C^{1}_{\rm b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is an
\alpha
-stable type process with
\alpha>1
, and

b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is bounded
and
\beta
-Hölder continuous in
x
uniformly in
t
with
\beta\in(\frac{2+\alpha}{2(1+\alpha)},1)
; this condition implies
\frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta>1
. The previous equation is different from our equation.
Similarly, in the case of Brownian motion
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, equation (
1.23
) with
L_{t}
replaced by
W_{t}

is not the equation considered in
[
39
]
, where
equation is formally like

\frac{\partial{u}(t,x,\omega)}{\partial t}+(b(t,x)+\frac{dW_{t}}{dt})\cdot Du(t,x,\omega)=0.

Remark 1.8
.

Let us consider the following possibly degenerate SDE with regular (Lipschitz) time-dependent coefficients

\displaystyle\xi_{s,t}(x)

\displaystyle=x+\int_{s}^{t}b(r,\xi_{s,r}(x))\,dr+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(r,\xi_{s,r-}(x))\tilde{N}(dr,dz),\;\;\;t\geq s;

(1.25)

see equation (3.4) and the proof of Theorem 3.1 in
[
61
]
. Here
\tilde{N}
is a compensated Poisson random measure on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

According to our discussion after (
1.4
), the existence of a solution flow associated with this equation is of utmost importance. For instance,
a natural question is whether there exists a function

\phi:[0,T]\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

where
\Omega^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{P}
-full event, such that
for every
(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the process
\big(\phi(s,t,x,\cdot):t\in[s,T]\big)

is a strong solution of Problem (
1.25
) and the sharp càdlàg
property (scp) is satisfied.
Property (scp) has not been stated in the recent monograph
[
62
]
by Kunita. On the other hand, it has been stated in the paper
[
61
]
but
the proof in
[
61
]
is not complete.

Indeed, on
[
61
, page 353
4-3
]
, it is stated,
but not proved
, that,
using notation
\xi_{t}:=\xi_{0,t}
, the inverse flow process
\xi_{t}^{-1}
is a well-defined
C(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
-valued càdlàg process. If this assertion were true, then we would be able to define the process satisfying the sharp
càdlàg property as follows:

\phi(s,t,x)=x
, if
t\leq s
and
\phi(s,t,x)=[\xi_{t}\circ\xi_{s}^{-1}](x)
, if
t\geq s
.

In this way, the claims at the beginning of
[
61
, Page 354]
would be completely justified.
However, we have not
found a proof of such a statement in either
[
61
]
or
[
62
]
. On the other hand,
the sharp càdlàg property has been recently proved by independent methods in a paper
[
15
]

by the second named author and Bondi for Lipschitz coefficients.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

By
\mathbb{N}
we will denote the set of all natural numbers starting from
0
. By
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
we will denote the set of those natural numbers which are
\geq 1
.
By
\mathbb{R}
we will denote the set of all real numbers. By
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
we will denote the set of those real numbers which are different from
0
and
by
\mathbb{R}^{+}
we will denote the set of those real numbers which are larger than
0
. By a countable set we will understand an infinite countable set, i.e., a set which is bijective with the set
\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathbb{Q}
be the set of all rational numbers in
\mathbb{R}
.

If
(X,\mathscr{X},\mu)
is a measure space, we call a subset
B
of
X
, a
\mu
-full set if and only if there exists a set
A\in\mathscr{X}
such that
A\subset B
and
\mu(X\setminus A)=0
.

Suppose that
(Y,|\cdot|_{Y})
is a normed vector space, e.g.,
Y=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
Y=\mathbb{R}
, then we will omit the symbol
Y
, e.g., we will write
C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
instead of
C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. A similar convention is also used for other function spaces.

Assume that
\beta\in(0,1)
. If
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we define semi-norms
\|\cdot\|_{0,K}
,
[\cdot]_{\beta,K}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\beta,K}
, by, for a function
f:K\to Y
,

\|f\|_{0,K}:=\sup\bigl\{|f(x)|_{Y}:\;x\in K\bigr\},

(2.1)

[f]_{\beta,K}:=\sup\Bigl\{\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|_{Y}}{|x-y|^{\beta}}:x,y\in K\text{ with }x\not=y\Bigr\},

(2.2)

and

\|f\|_{\beta,K}:=\|f\|_{0,K}+[f]_{\beta,K}.\\

(2.3)

By
C_{\mathrm{b}}(K,Y)
, we will denote the Banach space of all continuous and bounded functions
f:K\to Y
endowed with the supremum norm (
2.1
).
By
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}(K,Y)
we will denote the closed sub-space
C_{\mathrm{b}}(K,Y)
consisting of all
uniformly continuous functions.
If
K
is compact and
Y
is separable, then also the spaces
C_{\mathrm{b}}(K,Y)
and
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}(K,Y)
. If in addition
Y
is complete, then
these two spaces are separable Banach space as well.

By
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(K,Y)
we will denote the non-separable Banach space of all
bounded and
\beta
-Hölder continuous functions
f:K\to Y
endowed with the
norm (
2.3
).
Let us point out that the above definition makes sense also for
\beta=1
what corresponds to the Lipschitz condition. However,
the seminorms and normes in this case are defined differently, see below.

Let us also observe that
(
2.2
) makes sense for
\beta=0
and that

[f]_{0,K}\leq 2\|f\|_{0,K}.

If
K=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we will omit the subscript
K
from the notation introduced above.
By
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
we will denote the vector space of all infinitely differentiable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
Y
with compact support.
Note that if
\beta\in(0,1)
and
Y
is separable and complete, then the closure in
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
of the space

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
, denoted by
c^{\beta}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
, is a separable Banach space.

Similarly, if
n\geq 1
is a natural number, then for a suitable function
f:K\to Y
we put

\|f\|_{n,K}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{d}:|k|\leq n}\sup\,\bigl\{\|D^{k}f(x)\|:\;x\in K\bigr\}

(2.4)

and

\|f\|_{n+\beta,K}:=\|f\|_{n,K}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{d}:|k|=n}[D^{k}f]_{\beta,K}.

(2.5)

The last term on the RHS of (
2.5
) we will also denote by
\left[D^{n}f\right]_{\beta,K}
. As before, we omit the subscript
K
if
K=\mathbb{R}^{d}.

By
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
we will denote the separable Banach space of all

C^{n}
-class functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to Y
whose derivatives up to order
n
are bounded and uniformly continuous,
endowed with the
norm (
2.4
).
We also define

C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}C_{\mathrm{b}}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y).

If
n\geq 1
is a natural number and
\beta\in(0,1)
, then we say that a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to Y
belongs to
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{n+\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
if
f\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
and,
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
|k|=n

the partial derivatives
D^{k}f\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
. Note that
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{n+\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
is a Banach space endowed with the norm

\|\cdot\|_{n+\beta}
defined by (
2.5
).

In our paper we will also need the following fundamental results, see
[
76
, Lemma 4.1]
and inequality (4.16) therein. Our formulation of the first result is a bit more precise and a bit stronger. By
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
we denote the group of translation operators on the space
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
defined by

(\tau_{x}f)(y):=f(x+y),\;\;y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.6)

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume that
Y
is a normed vector space and
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
If
f\in C^{1+\gamma}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
and
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

|f(x+y)-f(y)-f(x+z)+f(z)|\leq[Df]_{\gamma}\,|y-z|\,|x|^{\gamma}.

(2.7)

If
\delta\in(0,\gamma]
,
f\in C^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

[\tau_{x}f-f]_{\delta}\leq 2|x|^{\gamma-\delta}[f]_{\gamma},

(2.8)

i.e., for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|f(x+y)-f(y)-f(x+z)+f(z)|\leq 2|x|^{\gamma-\delta}[f]_{\gamma}|y-z|^{\delta}.

(2.9)

Proof.

The proof of the first part proceeds by applying the Mean Value Theorem.
The proof of the second part is based on two observations: (i)
\tau_{x}
is a linear contraction in the space
C_{\mathrm{b}}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
; (ii)
\tau_{x}
is a linear contraction in the space
C^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},Y)
with respect to the seminorm
[\cdot]_{\gamma}
. Then (
2.8
) follows from a simple interpolation inequality (
2.10
) stated in Lemma
2.2
below and inequality

\displaystyle[\tau_{x}f-f]_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\tau_{x}f-f\|_{0}\leq 2|x|^{\gamma}[f]_{\gamma}.

∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Assume that
0\leq\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}\leq 1
and
\theta\in[0,1]
. Then, for every function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to Y
,

[f]_{(1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1}}\leq[f]_{\alpha_{0}}^{1-\theta}[f]_{\alpha_{1}}^{\theta}.

(2.10)

Proof of Lemma is simple and hence omitted.

∎

2.2
Lévy Process

In this subsection, we will recall the probabilistic framework from the paper
[
78
]
, see also
[
77
]
. The assumptions on the noise therein are weaker, i.e., more general, than those in the paper
[
76
]
.

Assume that
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},\mathbb{P})
is a probability space with a filtration
\mathbb{F}=\bigl({\mathscr{F}}_{t}\bigr)_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions.
Let
{\mathscr{P}}
be the predictable
\sigma
-field
on
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
, i.e., the
\sigma
-field generated by all left-continuous
\mathbb{F}
-adapted processes.

Let us recall that an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued process
L=(L_{t})_{\,t\geq 0}
is called a Lévy process if it has stationary and independent increments,
L_{0}=0
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. and all its sample paths are càdlàg. The last condition is often replaced by continuity in probability, see e.g.
[
75
, Definition 4.1 and Theorem 4.3]
. Often it is only required that the sample paths are càdlàg almost surely. By the Lévy-Khinchin formula, see
[
75
, Theorem 4.27]
and
[
82
, Section 11]
, a Lévy process can be characterized by the formula

\psi(\xi)=-{\rm{i}}\langle a,\xi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\xi,Q\xi\rangle-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(e^{{\rm{i}}\langle\xi,z\rangle}-1-{\rm{i}}\langle\xi,z\rangle\mathds{1}_{B}(z))\,\nu(dz),\ \ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.11)

where
\rm i=\sqrt{-1}
,
B
is the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,

B=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|z|\leq 1\},

(2.12)

(a,Q,\nu)
is a Lévy triplet of
L

and
\psi
is the symbol (called also exponent) of
L
, i.e.,

e^{-t\psi(\xi)}:=\mathbb{E}(e^{{\rm i}\langle\xi,L_{t}\rangle}),\;\;t\geq 0,\;\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.13)

A Lévy triplet
(a,Q,\nu)
consists of a vector
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, called the drift, a symmetric non-negative matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and a Lévy measure
\nu
, i.e., a non-negative Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\min\{1,|z|^{2}\}\,\nu(dz)<\infty.

(2.14)

In the above and in what follows, we use the notation we have learned about from the paper
[
58
]
:

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{C}f(z)\,\nu(dz):=\int_{C\setminus\{0\}}f(z)\,\nu(dz),\;\;C\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Let us recall the
Lévy-Itô decomposition of the process
L
with Lévy triplet
(a,Q,\nu)
, see
[
4
, Theorem 2.4.16]
or

[
61
, Theorem 2.7]
, according to which, there exists
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a Brownian motion
W_{Q}
with covariance matrix
Q
and an independent Poisson random measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
such that, for each
t\geq 0
,

L_{t}=a\,t+W_{Q}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{B}z\tilde{\mu}(ds,dz)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B^{\mathrm{c}}}z\mu(ds,dz),\;\;t\geq 0,

(2.15)

where we adopt the notation as before
\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{B}z\tilde{\mu}(ds,dz)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B\setminus\{0\}}z\tilde{\mu}(ds,dz)
.
Note that
\tilde{\mu}
is the corresponding compensated Poisson random measure, i.e.,

\tilde{\mu}:=\mu-\mathrm{Leb}\otimes\nu.

(2.16)

In this paper, we will consider, as in
[
77
]
, a pure jump
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued Lévy process
L=(L_{t})_{\,t\geq 0}
without the drift term, i.e., with Lévy triplet
(0,0,\nu)
. In other words, the Lévy process
L
can be represented in the following form

\displaystyle L_{t}=L(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{B}z\tilde{\mu}(ds,dz)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{B^{\mathrm{c}}}z\mu(ds,dz),\;\;t\geq 0.

(2.17)

Definition 2.3
.

The Markov semigroup
({\mathscr{R}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on
C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and associated to the process
L
is defined by

{\mathscr{R}}_{t}f(x)=\mathbb{E}(f(x+L_{t})),\ t\geq 0,\,f\in C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.18)

The pre-generator
{\mathscr{A}}
of the semigroup
({\mathscr{R}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}}g(x)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(g(x+z)-g(x)-\mathds{1}_{B}(z)\langle z,Dg(x)\rangle)\,\nu(dz),\;\;g\in\mathcal{D}({\mathscr{A}}),

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}({\mathscr{A}})

\displaystyle=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where by
Dg(x)
we denote the gradient of the function
g
at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
Dg(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unique vector such that

(Dg(x),y)=(d_{x}g)(y),\;\;y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.20)

where
d_{x}g\in{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Fréchet derivative of
g
at
x
.

Obviously
{\mathscr{R}}_{0}=I
and the words “Markov semigroup” can be justified by showing that the family
{\mathscr{R}}=\bigl({\mathscr{R}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is indeed a
positivity preserving strongly continuous contraction semigroup on the Banach space
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which by definition is the (closed) subspace of
C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
consisting of those functions belonging to the latter space which vanish at
\infty
.
Note that we have not characterised the domain of the infinitesimal generator of this semigroup.

Remark 2.4
.

One can prove, see the discussion at the beginning of Section 4 of
[
77
]
or bottom of page 427 of
[
76
]
, that
the RHS of formula (
2.19
)
makes sense for
g\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, if
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
. Moreover, the resulting function belongs to
C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and it will also be denoted by
{\mathscr{A}}g
.

2.3
Hypotheses on the noise in the transport equation

We consider an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued purely jump Lévy process
L
with a Lévy measure
\nu
. Let us define the Blumenthal-Getoor index of the process
L
, see
[
14
]
, by

\alpha:=\inf\Big\{\sigma>0:\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{B}|y|^{\sigma}\nu(dy)<\infty\Big\}.

(2.21)

We assume that
\alpha\in(0,2)
.

We denote by
e_{1},...,e_{d}
the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and denote, cf.
[
39
]
,

L_{t}=(L_{t}^{1},...,L_{t}^{d}),\;\;t\geq 0.

(2.22)

We consider a vector field

b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

(2.23)

which is
\beta
-Hölder continuous and bounded,
\beta\in(0,1)
, i.e.,

b\in C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.24)

We assume as in
[
76
,
77
,
78
]
and
[
30
]
that

\frac{\alpha}{2}+\beta>1.

(2.25)

In some parts of this paper we will make an additional assumption that
the distributional divergence of
b
exists and is locally integrable, i.e.,

\operatorname{div}b\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.26)

In this paper we will
study two objects. Firstly, we are interested in the following stochastic differential equation (SDE) driven by process
L

\displaystyle dX_{t}

\displaystyle=b(X_{t})\,dt+dL_{t},\;t>0.\;\;\;

(2.27)

Secondly, we are interested in the following stochastic partial differential equation of transport type

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{u(t,x)}}{\partial t}+b(x)\cdot Du(t,x)\,+\sum_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}u(t-,x)\diamond\frac{dL_{t}^{i}}{dt}=0,~t\in[0,T];

(2.28)

\displaystyle u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\ \ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2.29)

where
D_{i}u=e_{i}\cdot Du
is the derivative in the
i
-th coordinate direction and the initial data
u_{0}
is a bounded Borel function and the stochastic integration is understood in the Marcus form, see e.g.,
[
4
]
and
[
80
, page 82]
. Let us point out that equation (
2.27
) is the characteristics equation for the transport equation
(
1.1
) or (
2.28
).

Following
[
78
]
, see also
[
77
]
), we assume

Hypothesis 1
.

The Lévy process
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
has a Blumenthal-Getoor index
\alpha\in(0,2)
.

The Markov semigroup
({\mathscr{R}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies
{\mathscr{R}}_{t}(C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\subset C^{1}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for every
t>0
and there exists
c=c_{\alpha}>0
such that, for every
f\in C_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D({\mathscr{R}}_{t}f)(x)|\leq\frac{c}{t^{1/\alpha}}\;\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|,\;\;\;t\in(0,1].

(2.30)

There exists
\theta>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\{|z|>1\}}|z|^{\theta}\nu(dz)<\infty.

(2.31)

Hypothesis
1
is the most general assumption we impose on the noise, compared with Hypotheses
2
and
3
below. Note that we always have
\alpha\in[0,2]
.
In particular, assumptions
(H)
and
(R)
are equivalent to the assumptions in
[
77
]
when
\alpha\in(0,2)
.
Assumption
(T)
will be used in the whole paper starting from Theorem
3.5
. For examples of Lévy processes that satisfy Hypothesis
1
, please refer to
[
78
]
.

In the paper we will consider Hypothesis
1
in the case of
\alpha\in[1,2)
. Indeed it is even not known if pathwise uniqueness holds for the corresponding SDE
under Hypothesis
1
when
\alpha\in(0,1)
and condition (
2.25
) holds; cf. Remarks
2.5
and
3.3
.

Hypothesis
1
in particular will imply existence and uniqueness of weak solution to the transport equation when
\alpha\geq 1
, see Theorem
6.18
, which only requires
b\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, provided that
1>\beta>1-\frac{\alpha}{4}>\frac{1}{2}
and
\operatorname{div}b\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
when
d\geq 1
.

For the first crucial uniqueness results, see Theorem
6.16
in
Section
6
, we will need the following stronger assumption on the noise. In Theorem
6.16
, we assume weaker Hölder regularity condition
b\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
1-\frac{\alpha}{2}<\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, in addition an
L^{p}
integrability condition on the divergence
\operatorname{div}b\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
p\geq 2
when
d\geq 1
.

Hypothesis 2
.

The Lévy process
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a symmetric
\alpha
-stable process, which is non-degenerate, i.e.,

\mbox{ there exists
$C_{\alpha}>0$: }\;\;\psi(u)\geq C_{\alpha}|u|^{\alpha},\;\;u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.32)

Again, we always consider Hypothesis
2
with
\alpha\in[1,2)
. We will use this hypothesis as needed to apply
L^{p}
-estimates, see in particular
Theorem
6.10
. Hypothesis
2
is a special case of the assumptions used in
[
95
]
. Indeed we do not know if the results from
[
95
]
hold when
0<\alpha<1
.

We have a final assumption which is the strongest one. We will apply it in Theorem
6.18
to show the existence (and even the uniqueness) of a weak solution when
0<\alpha<1
.

Hypothesis 3
.

The Lévy process
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a symmetric
\alpha
-stable process, which is rotationally invariant, i.e., there exists a positive
constant

C_{\alpha}
such that, for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\psi(u)=C_{\alpha}|u|^{\alpha}.

(2.33)

Remark 2.5
.

In paper
[
77
]
, the second named author proved that
if
\alpha\in[1,2)
,
L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}

verifies
(H)
and
(R)
in Hypothesis
1

and

b\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\beta
satisfying condition (
2.25
),
then the existence and the strong uniqueness holds for the following SDE with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
s\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle dX_{t}

\displaystyle=b(X_{t})\,dt+dL_{t},\;t>s;\;\;\;

(2.34)

\displaystyle X_{s}

\displaystyle=x.

(2.35)

In the earlier paper

[
76
]
, he proved similar statements under an additional assumptions that

L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
verifies Hypothesis
2
with
\alpha\in[1,2)
.
Indeed all the results from
[
76
]
have been generalized in
[
77
]

by introducing new weaker assumptions
(H)
and
(R)
of Hypothesis
1
.

A sufficient condition in order that condition
(R)
in Hypothesis
1
holds is the following one, see also
[
83
]
.

There exists a Lévy measure
\nu_{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and there exist
C_{1},C_{2},M>0
such that symbol
\psi_{1}
corresponding to
\nu_{1}
, i.e., defined by

\psi_{1}(\xi)=-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}}\kern-7.08333pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}}\kern-5.20834pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.4358pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}}\kern-4.28824pt}}\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(e^{{\rm i}\langle\xi,z\rangle}-1-{\rm i}\langle\xi,z\rangle\mathds{1}_{B}(z))\,\nu_{1}(dz),\;\;\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

satisfies the following inequality

C_{1}|\xi|^{\alpha}\leq Re\,\psi_{1}(\xi)\leq C_{2}|\xi|^{\alpha},\quad|\xi|>M,

(2.36)

and such that

\nu(A)\geq\nu_{1}(A),\;\;A\in{\mathscr{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Note that in paper
[
78
]
a stronger result is proven but under an additional assumption
(T)
, see Theorem
3.5
below.

Before we state the next important inequality, let us add some comments regarding notations we use in our paper.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
C\in{\mathscr{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
and consider a
{\mathscr{P}}\otimes{\mathscr{B}}(C)/{\mathscr{B}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-measurable mapping

F:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\times C\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.37)

If

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{C}|F(s,z)|^{2}\nu(dz)ds<\infty,\mbox{ for every }T>0,

(2.38)

then one can define
the stochastic integral

Z_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{C}F(s,z)\tilde{\mu}(ds,dz),\;\;t\in[0,\infty),

(2.39)

and the process
Z=(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is an
L^{2}
-martingale with a càdlàg modification.

The construction of
the Itô stochastic integral can also be done by defining the stochastic integral first for predictable integrands, and then extending
the stochastic integral to progressively measurable integrands using the fact that the dual predictable
projection of a Poisson random measure, is absolutely continuous with respect
to the Lebesgue measure (with respect to the time variable).

We need the following fundamental
L^{p}
-inequality (
2.40
) for stochastic integrals, see
[
61
, Theorem 2.11]
for more details. This inequality is usually called the Burkholder inequality.

Proposition 2.7
.

For every
p\geq 2
,
there exists
c(p)>0
such that for every process
F
as in Proposition
2.6
and for every
T\geq 0
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}|Z_{s}|^{p}\Big]\leq c(p)\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{C}|F(s,z)|^{2}\nu(dz)ds\Bigr)^{p/2}+\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{C}|F(s,z)|^{p}\nu(dz)ds\right].

(2.40)

Of course, the above result is interesting only if the RHS of inequality (
2.40
) is finite.

3
A regular stochastic flow

In this section, we consider a vector field
b
as in (
2.23
) which only satisfies the condition (
2.24
).
We will not use the assumption (
2.26
) about the divergence of
b
.

Let us first recall the following result from
[
77
, Theorem 1.1]
, see also
[
76
]
. Note that these results hold also in the case when
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is Lipschitz and bounded, i.e., when
\beta=1
.

We comment on the difference between the next theorem and
Theorem
1.6
in Remark
3.4
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Assume that
\alpha\in[1,2)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
satisfy condition
(
2.25
). Assume that
b\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Assume that
L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Lévy process satisfying
(H)
and
(R)
in Hypothesis
1
.
Then the global existence and the pathwise uniqueness hold for Problem (
2.34
)-(
2.35
).

Moreover, if
X^{x}=(X_{t}^{x})_{t\geq 0}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denotes
the unique solution of Problem (
2.34
)-(
2.35
) with
s=0
, then the following conditions are satisfied:

(i) for all
T>0
and
p\geq 1
, there exists
a constant
C(p,T)>0
(depending also on
\alpha,\beta,d
, the Lévy measure
\nu
and the norm
\|b\|_{\beta}
) such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\,\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}|X_{s}^{x}-X_{s}^{y}|^{p}\,\Big]\,\leq C(p,T)\,\,|x-y|^{p},\;\;\;x,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d};

(3.1)

(ii) for every
t\geq 0
, the mapping

\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto X_{t}^{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.2)

is a surjective homeomorphism,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.;

(iii) for every
t\geq 0
, the mapping

\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto X_{t}^{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is a
C^{1}
-function

\mathbb{P}
-a.s..

When
\alpha\in(0,1)
a related result follows from Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.4 in
[
30
]
. Indeed, we have:

Theorem 3.2
.

Assume that
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
satisfy condition
(
2.25
).
Assume that
b\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Assume that
L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Lévy process satisfying Hypothesis
3
.
Then assertions (i), (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
3.1
continue to hold.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that the assumptions of Theorem 1.2 in
[
30
]
are more general than
those of Theorem
3.2
. On the other hand, it remains an
Open Problem
if the pathwise uniqueness holds for Problem (
2.34
)-(
2.35
) with
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
satisfying condition
(
2.25
) and
L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Lévy process satisfying
(H)
and
(R)
in the general Hypothesis
1
(cf. Remark
2.5
). On this respect we mention
[
23
]
which also contains
pathwise uniqueness results when
0<\alpha<1
under additional hypotheses.

Remark 3.4
.

The proofs of Theorem
3.1
given in
[
77
]
and Theorem
3.2
given in
[
30
]

show that the assertions (ii) and (iii) of Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
hold on a
\mathbb{P}
-full set which can possibly depend on
t\geq 0
.
We could rewrite these conditions (ii) and (iii) as follows.

For any
t\geq 0
, there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-full event

\Omega_{t}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{F}}:\;\;\;\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{t}^{\prime})=1,

(3.3)

such that, for every
\omega\in\Omega_{t}^{\prime}
, the map

\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto X_{t}^{x}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.4)

is a surjective homeomorphism
of
C^{1}
-class.

However, the
C^{1}
-class diffeomorphism property of the map from (
3.2
) has not been investigated.

According to the assertions in Theorem
1.6
relevant goals
of the present Section
3
are to show that
\Omega_{t}^{\prime}
can be chosen independent of
t
, see Theorem
3.8
, and to
demonstrate the
C^{1}
-class diffeomorphism property of the map starting from (
3.2
), see Theorem
3.23
.

3.1
The homeomorphism property

We start with
[
78
, Theorem 6.8]

and
[
78
, Theorem 6.10]
which are consolidated in the next theorem (a related result which covers degenerate SDEs is
[
79
, Theorem 3.5]
).

Note that in
[
78
]
the vector field
b
was time-dependent and the time
t
was restricted to a bounded interval
[0,T]
. Since we consider the vector field
b
here to be time-independent, we can work on the whole unbounded interval
[0,\infty)
. We only have to pay attention to estimates which are uniform only on bounded subsets of the set
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

In the next result we need all the assumptions of Hypothesis
1
(including
(T)
).

Theorem 3.5
.

Assume that
\beta\in(0,1)
satisfies condition
(
2.25
) with
b\in C_{\mathrm{b}}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Moreover, if
\alpha\in[1,2)
, then we assume that
L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Lévy process satisfying Hypothesis
1
, i.e.,

(H)
,
(R)
and
(T)
. If
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then we assume that
L
satisfies
Hypothesis
3
.

Then, there exists a
{\mathscr{B}}([0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\otimes{\mathscr{F}}/{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
-measurable function

\phi:[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

(3.5)

such that for every
(s,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the process
\big(\phi(s,t,x,\cdot)\big)_{t\in[s,\infty)}

is a strong solution of Problem (
2.34
)-(
2.35
).

Moreover, there exists an almost sure event
\Omega^{\prime}

such that

the following assertions hold for every
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
.

(i) For any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the mapping

[0,\infty)\ni s\mapsto\phi(s,t,x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is càdlàg locally uniformly in
t
and
x
, i.e.,
if
s\in(0,\infty)
and
(s_{k})
is a sequence such that
s_{k}\nearrow s
,
then, for all
T,M>0
, the following hold

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{|x|\leq M}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\phi(s_{k},t,x,\omega)-\phi(s-,t,x,\omega)|=0,

(3.6)

and if
s\in[0,\infty)
and

(s^{n})
is sequence such that
s^{n}\searrow s
, then, for all
T,M>0
, the following holds

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{|x|\leq M}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\phi(s^{n},t,x,\omega)-\phi(s,t,x,\omega)|=0.

(3.7)

(ii) For all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
s\in[0,\infty)
,

\phi(s,t,x,\omega)=x,\quad\mbox{ if }\,0\leq t\leq s,

(3.8)

and

\phi(s,t,x,\omega)=x+\int_{s}^{t}b\left(\phi(s,r,x,\omega)\right)dr+L_{t}(\omega)-L_{s}(\omega),\;\;\;t\in[s,\infty).

(3.9)

(iii) For any
s\in[0,\infty)
, the function

\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\phi(s,t,x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is continuous in
x
, locally uniformly with respect to
t
.

Moreover,
for every integer
n>2d
and every
T>0
, there exists a
{\mathscr{B}}([0,T])\otimes{\mathscr{F}}/{\mathscr{B}}([0,\infty])
-measurable function
V_{n}(\cdot,\cdot;T):[0,T]\times\Omega\to[0,\infty]
such that

\int_{0}^{T}V_{n}(s,\omega;T)\,ds<\infty

(3.10)

and

\displaystyle\hskip-170.71652pt\sup_{t\in[0,T]}

\displaystyle|\phi(s,t,x,\omega)-\phi(s,t,y,\omega)|

(3.11)

\displaystyle\leq V_{n}(s,\omega;T)\,|x-y|^{\frac{n-2d}{n}}[(|x|\vee|y|)^{\frac{2d+1}{n}}\vee 1],\;\;\;x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;s\in[0,T].

(iv) For any
0\leq s<r\leq t<\infty
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\phi(s,t,x,\omega)=\phi(r,t,\phi(s,r,x,\omega),\omega),

(3.12)

i.e.,

\phi_{s,t}=\phi_{r,t}\circ\phi_{s,r},

(3.13)

where

\phi_{s,t}(x,\omega):=\phi(s,t,x,\omega).

(3.14)

(v)
If for some
s_{0}\in[0,\infty)
,
\tau=\tau(\omega)\in(s_{0},\infty)
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, a càdlàg function

g:[s_{0},\tau)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
solves the integral equation

g(t)=x+\int_{s_{0}}^{t}b\left(g(r)\right)dr+L_{t}(\omega)-L_{s_{0}}(\omega),\;\;\;t\in[s_{0},\tau),

(3.15)

then we have
g(r)=\phi(s_{0},r,x,\omega)
, for
r\in[s_{0},\tau)
.

In the present paper we prove new results which extend the results from Theorem
3.5
, see e.g. Lemma
3.6
and Theorems
3.8
,

3.19
and
3.23
.

In the sequel
\Omega^{\prime}
(or
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}
) will denote a
\mathbb{P}
-full event that can be obtained starting from the set of full measure given in Theorem
3.5
. For each
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see Theorem
3.5
, we set

\phi_{s,t}(x)(\omega):=\phi_{s,t}(x,\omega)=\phi(s,t,x,\omega),\;\;\;s,t\in[0,\infty).

(3.16)

We can extend the random variables
\phi_{s,t}(x)
from
\Omega^{\prime}
to the whole of
\Omega
, by defining them to be equal to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(x):=x
when
\omega\in\Omega\setminus\Omega^{\prime}
.

By Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
we know that, for every
t\in[0,\infty)
, there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-full event possibly depending on
t
such that on this event
we have that
\phi_{0,t}(x)
is a homeomorphism.
We extend this property
showing that

Lemma 3.6
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
3.5
,
there exists an almost sure event
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}
such that
for every
\omega\in\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}
and every
t\in[0,\infty)
,
the mapping

\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\phi_{0,t}(x)(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is a surjective homeomorphism.

Moreover, on
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}
, for every
T\geq 0
, we have:

\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(x)=\phi_{0,T}^{-1}(\phi_{t,T}(x)),\;\;\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ t\in[0,T].

(3.17)

Here and in the remainder of this paper, we denote by
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
the inverse of
\phi_{0,t}
. Finally, on
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}
, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the process

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\phi_{0,t}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.18)

is càdlàg.

Proof.

The definition of
\phi_{0,t}
,
t\geq 0
implies that the process
X=(X_{t}:=\phi_{0,t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies conditions (i), (ii), and (iii) in Theorem
3.1
.
In order to go further we
fix
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

According to assertions (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
3.1
, the mapping
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\rightarrow\phi_{0,T}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a surjective homeomorphism and a
C^{1}
-function on a
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\Omega^{T}
which is only dependent on
T
. Meanwhile, by assertion (iii) of Theorem
3.5
, for any
t\geq 0
, the function
\phi_{t,T}(x)
is continuous w.r.t.
x
on some
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\Omega^{\prime}
, which is introduced in Theorem
3.5
and independent of
t\in[0,\infty)
. Hence, we have that on
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\Omega_{0}^{T}=\Omega^{T}\cap\Omega^{\prime}
, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
the
random variable

\eta_{0,t}(x):=\phi_{0,T}^{-1}(\phi_{t,T}(x)),

is well defined, depends continuously on
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Furthermore, by using assertion (i) of Theorem
3.5
together with the continuity of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\phi_{0,T}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we infer that the process

[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\eta_{0,t}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.19)

is càdlàg.

We also have, using (
3.12
),

\eta_{0,t}(\phi_{0,t}(x))=\phi_{0,T}^{-1}(\phi_{t,T}(\phi_{0,t}(x)))=x.

(3.20)

This shows that
\phi_{0,t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
one to one
and that
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}=\eta_{0,t}
.

Let us prove the
surjectivity
of
\phi_{0,t}
. Let us choose and fix
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T)
. By Theorem
3.1
, we infer that for every
q\in[0,T]\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\Omega_{q}
and
x_{q}
such that

\phi_{0,q}(x_{q})=y,\quad\text{on }\Omega_{q}.

(3.21)

Set

\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}_{T}=\Omega^{T}_{0}\cap(\mathop{\cap}\limits_{q\in[0,T]\cap{\mathbb{Q}}}\Omega_{q}).

(3.22)

Clearly
\mathbb{P}(\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}_{T})=1
.
Let us choose a sequence
(t_{n})\subset[0,T]\cap{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
t_{n}\searrow t

from the right

and write
x_{t_{n}}=x_{n}
. Note that on
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}_{T}
we have, see (ii) in Theorem
3.5
,

\phi_{0,t_{n}}(x_{n})=x_{n}+\int_{0}^{t_{n}}b(\phi_{0,s}(x_{n}))\,ds+L_{t_{n}}.

(3.23)

By the boundedness of
b
, it follows that, for every
{n}\geq 1
,
|x_{n}|\leq M_{T}(\omega)+|y|
, since
\phi_{0,t_{n}}(x_{n})=y
and
L
is càdlàg.

Hence, by possibly passing to a subsequence we may assume that there exists
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
x_{n}\to z
. Passing to the limit in (
3.21
), we obtain using also assertions (ii) and (iii) in Theorem
3.5
,

|\phi_{0,{t_{n}}}(x_{n})-\phi_{0,{t}}(z)|\leq|\phi_{0,{t_{n}}}(x_{n})-\phi_{0,{t_{n}}}(z)|+|\phi_{0,{t_{n}}}(z)-\phi_{0,{t}}(z)|\to 0\;\;\text{as}\;{n}\to\infty.

(3.24)

Hence

\phi_{0,t}(z)=y.

This shows that
\phi_{0,t}
is onto for
t\in[0,T)
.

Now set
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}=\cap_{T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}_{T}
. Since
by the previous arguments, for each
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
is well define for all
t\in[0,T]
on the
\mathbb{P}
-full event
\bar{\Omega}_{T}^{\prime}
, it follows that for every
t\geq 0
(which lies in some interval
[0,T]
,
T\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
),
\phi_{0,t}^{-1}
is well defined on
\bar{\Omega}^{\prime}
